# APPENDIX Z: Certificate-Conditional Interface Ledger, Fine-Structure Candidate, and Euclidean Response-Carrier Dimension

## Overview Structure

This appendix derives the minimal interface ledger $M=24$ on the stated Hilbert-carrier, binary-record, active-projector, and PCE-attractor branches. It then:
- evaluates a scheme-specified Thomson-limit core candidate on the Ward, interface-response, curvature, projection, and transport model branches of Sections Z.24--Z.26;
- proves the least-feasible Euclidean response-carrier dimension on the Bures tangent-cell branch of Theorems Z.10--Z.11, with physical spacetime promotion separately certificate-gated by Corollary P.8.3.

The core arithmetic is deductive after its independent model and normalization inputs are fixed. Identification with the physical Thomson coupling requires an operator/current realization and a complete residual certificate; the diagnostic hypercharge-recoil construction does not yet supply those gates.

### Z.1.5 The Grassmannian as Geometric Arena

The orbit of the rank-two reference state is the mathematical homogeneous space
$$
\mathcal O_{\rho_0}
\cong U(8)/[U(2)\times U(6)]
\cong \operatorname{Gr}(2,8).
$$
This orbit fixes a candidate carrier and its dimensions. It does not, by itself, select a physical Hamiltonian, an $E_8$ embedding, flavor labels, response functions, or Standard Model parameters.

**Proposition Z.1.5 (Grassmannian Carrier and Parameter-Ledger Separation).** *On the minimal active-projector branch, the reference-state orbit is $\operatorname{Gr}(2,8)$, with complex dimension $12$ and real dimension $24$. These carrier identities do not by themselves determine an $E_8$ embedding, flavor labels, mass ratios, CP phases, a realized generation multiplicity, an electromagnetic response coefficient, or Standard Model parameters.*

*Proof.* The Grassmannian arises from the derivation chain
$$K_0 = 3 \xrightarrow{\text{Thm 23}} d_0 \ge 8 \xrightarrow{\text{Thm Z.2 on the minimal PCE branch}} d_0 = 8 \xrightarrow{\text{Thm Z.1}} a = 2, b = 6 \xrightarrow{\text{Def Z.3}} \rho_0 = \tfrac{1}{2}I_2 \oplus 0_6.$$
Consequently,
$$
\mathcal{O}_{\rho_0}
=
\{U\rho_0U^\dagger:U\in U(8)\}
\cong
\operatorname{Gr}(2,8),
$$
and
$$
\dim_{\mathbb C}\operatorname{Gr}(2,8)=ab=12,
\qquad
\dim_{\mathbb R}\operatorname{Gr}(2,8)=2ab=24.
$$
The numerical coincidence with the Golay signal dimension uses the separate predictive-recovery branch of Theorem Z.13. The hierarchy law of Theorem T.39, the flavor-triad data and scale map, the base holonomy of Theorem T.54, the nonlinear phase response of Theorem T.56, the exact family realization of Proposition R.3.5.1a, and the visible-response coefficient of Theorem Z.24 are additional branch data. Hence the orbit and its dimensions are established, while the listed physical parameter ledgers are not consequences of the orbit alone. ∎

**Remark Z.1.5.1: Shared Carrier Is Not Parameter Derivation.** The same Grassmannian can support the conditional coupling, hierarchy, and flavor constructions used later in the manuscript. This shared carrier does not identify their response maps or discharge their independent branch hypotheses.

## Z.2 MPU Invariants and the Active Kernel

### Z.2.1 Foundational Constants Recap
- **Hilbert space dimension:** On Theorem 15's (O1)–(O3), (FC) class with mutually perfectly distinguishable Hilbert representatives, Theorem 23 gives $d_0\ge8$; equality requires the additional quotientability, strict-cost, admissible-comparator, and minimal-branch hypotheses of Theorem Z.2.
- **Structural binary reference:** Definition 28 and Theorem J.1 give $\varepsilon_0=\ln2$ as the log-cardinality of the registered reachable binary verification quotient.
- **Registered physical reset:** Theorem 31 gives $\varepsilon_{\mathrm{reset}}\ge H_q(P\mid R)$; equality requires a separately registered overhead-free Landauer implementation, and a positive floor requires a lower bound on $H_q(P\mid R)$.


- **Horizon constant:** $K_0=3$ is the least visited-context log-capacity on Theorem 15's (O1)–(O3), (FC) realization class.

### Z.2.2 The Principle of Physical Instantiation (PPI)
- Abstract logical costs must manifest as actual physical systems
- Definition P.6.2 from Appendix P

### Z.2.3 Structural Binary Active-Kernel Selection

**Theorem Z.1 (Structural Binary Active-Kernel Selection on a Same-Response Minimal Branch).** Assume (i) the registered sharp match/mismatch quotient and entropy-capacity gate; (ii) a nonempty comparison class in which the required protocol-response presheaf and predictive performance are fixed; (iii) an admissible $a=2$ implementation of that same response; and (iv) after response and performance are fixed, maintained active-support dimension has strictly increasing positive cost and no other ledger term improves with unused support. Then the unique least-support representative has active-kernel dimension


$$
a = 2
$$

*Proof.*

**Step 1 (Registered noninjective reset branch).** On the declared reachable binary verification-quotient branch with the prescribed ready-state reset of Lemma Z.2, the accessible completed-cycle map is noninjective. If that branch performs a registered isothermal reset, Theorem 31 gives
$$
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{reset}}\ge H_q(P\mid R).
$$
The structural binary quotient has log-cardinality $\varepsilon_0=\ln2$; SPAP alone does not activate this reset or force the physical entropy to equal $\ln2$. PCE uses the structural reference in the discrete no-surplus comparison.



**Step 2 (Active verification record).** The active kernel is the Hilbert carrier of the retained SPAP verification record, not an arbitrary yes/no effect. A faithful comparison has two possible sharp retained values, match and mismatch. On the Hilbert-carrier branch, two sharp retained alternatives require two orthogonal rays. Hence
$$
a\ge2.
$$

**Step 3 (Entropy-capacity admissibility).** For an $a$-dimensional physical subsystem, the von Neumann entropy satisfies
$$
S(\rho)\le \ln a,
$$
with equality only for the maximally mixed state. To instantiate the structural SPAP quotient inside the active verification kernel, PPI admissibility requires
$$
\ln a\ge\varepsilon_0=\ln2.
$$
This also gives $a\ge2$ for integer Hilbert dimension $a$.

**Step 4 (Same-response minimality and uniqueness).** Steps 2--3 give $a\ge2$. Hypothesis (iii) proves feasibility at $a=2$. For every comparator with $a>2$ in the same fixed-response and fixed-performance class, hypothesis (iv) gives strictly greater descended cost, so it cannot minimize. A larger carrier whose extra directions change a retained response or predictive performance is not a comparator in this theorem; it is a separate branch and is not excluded by no-surplus reasoning. Therefore the unique least-support representative in the registered comparison class has $a=2$. ∎

**Remark: Axiomatic Status.** This derivation uses exactly:
- Registered binary verification/reset branch: Lemma Z.2 supplies a 2-to-1 merge only under its reachability and ready-state-reset hypotheses; SPAP alone does not force that merge.
- Landauer's principle (Physics): implies $\varepsilon_{\mathrm{reset}}\ge H_q(P\mid R)\quad\text{on a registered reset branch}$; equality requires the overhead-free implementation branch and is not selected by PCE alone.


- The active verification record: requires two sharp retained alternatives and hence $a\ge2$ on the Hilbert-carrier branch.
- PPI (Definition P.6.2): requires finite physical instantiation of the structural entropy quotient by the retained active kernel.
- Entropy capacity bound: gives $S(\rho)\le \ln a$.
- PCE no-surplus selection: removes response-null active alternatives and rejects larger active support on the minimal branch.

Thus $a=2$ is theorem-level only on this same-response, admissible-comparator, strict-support-cost branch; it is not fixed by SPAP, entropy capacity, or response-null quotienting alone.

### Z.2.4 The PPI → 24 Derivation Chain

The complete chain to $M = 24$ with no continuous fit parameters is:

The structural and physical ledgers are parallel:
$$
\boxed{\text{declared binary verification quotient}
\xrightarrow{\text{Def 28; Def J.1; Thm J.1}}
\varepsilon_0=\ln2
\xrightarrow{\text{Thm Z.1}}
a=2
\xrightarrow[\text{Thm Z.2}]{d_0=8\text{ on the minimal PCE branch}}
M=2ab=24,}
$$
while a physical implementation that closes a registered reset separately obeys
$$
\boxed{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{reset}}\ge H_q(P\mid R)}
$$
by Theorem 31.

| Step | Result | Justification | Status |
|------|--------|---------------|--------|
| 1 | Structural reference $\varepsilon_0=\ln2$ | Registered binary verification quotient | Definition 28; Theorem J.1 |
| 2 | $\varepsilon_{\mathrm{reset}}\ge H_q(P\mid R)$ on a registered reset branch | Distribution-sensitive Landauer reset ledger | Theorem 31 |


| 3 | $d_0 \ge 8$ | Capacity-dimension link for $K_0=3$ | Theorem 23 |
| 4 | $d_0=8$ on the registered comparator branch | Hilbert lower bound plus an admissible same-class eight-dimensional comparator, response-preserving quotientability, strict maintained-support cost, and minimality | Theorem Z.2 |
| 5 | $a = 2$ | Sharp match/mismatch record $a\ge2$ + admissibility $\ln a \ge \varepsilon_0$ + PPI/PCE no-surplus optimality | Theorem Z.1 |
| 6 | $b = d_0 - a = 6$ | Arithmetic on the minimal branch | Theorem Z.1; Theorem Z.2 |
| 7 | $M = 2ab = 24$ | QFI mode count on the interface | Theorem Z.5 |

Thus $M = 24$ follows from the axioms together with the explicit discrete branch assumptions already stated in the appendix. No continuous parameter is fitted at any stage.

### Z.2.5 The PCE-Attractor Density Operator

On the minimal active-projector branch, the PCE reference state is

$$\rho_0 = \frac{1}{a} I_a \oplus 0_b = \frac{1}{2} I_2 \oplus 0_6.$$

Its entropy on its support is
$$S(\rho_0|_A)=-\operatorname{tr}_A\left(\frac{I_a}{a}\ln\frac{I_a}{a}\right)=\ln a=\ln2.$$
Thus the active-state entropy equals the structural quotient $\varepsilon_0=\ln2$ on this branch. This is a state-counting identity, not the physical equality $\varepsilon_{\mathrm{phys}}=H_q(P\mid R)$:

$$\ln a=\varepsilon_0.$$

The interface-direction QFI spectrum of this stipulated state is computed in Theorem Z.5. No state-space maximization or uniqueness claim follows from the entropy identity alone.

**Definition Z.2.5a (Peirce Active-Projector Ledger).** Let
$$
E=\mathbb C^8,\qquad
p=\begin{pmatrix}I_2&0\\0&0_6\end{pmatrix}
$$
be the support projector of the active component of $\rho_0$. The Peirce decomposition of $E$ is
$$
E=pE\oplus(1-p)E
=
\mathbb C^2\oplus\mathbb C^6.
\tag{Z.2.5a.1}
$$
The Peirce off-diagonal predictive-response sector is
$$
\mathcal P_{\mathrm{off}}
=
\operatorname{Hom}(pE,(1-p)E).
\tag{Z.2.5a.2}
$$

**Theorem Z.2.5b (Peirce-Grassmannian Minimal Integer Ledger).** On the minimal active-projector branch,
$$
(a,b,q_{\mathrm{geom}},M)=(2,6,12,24).
\tag{Z.2.5b.1}
$$
More explicitly,
$$
a=\dim_\mathbb C pE=2,
\qquad
b=\dim_\mathbb C(1-p)E=6,
$$
$$
q_{\mathrm{geom}}
:=\dim_\mathbb C\operatorname{Hom}(pE,(1-p)E)
=2\cdot6=12,
$$
and
$$
M=\dim_\mathbb R T_p\mathrm{Gr}(2,8)
=2\dim_\mathbb C\operatorname{Hom}(pE,(1-p)E)
=24.
$$

*Proof.* Since $p$ has rank $2$ on $E=\mathbb C^8$, its image has complex dimension $2$ and its complement has complex dimension $6$. The tangent space is canonically
$$
T_p\mathrm{Gr}(2,8)\cong\operatorname{Hom}(pE,(1-p)E),
$$
so its complex and real dimensions are $12$ and $24$. These are the active, inactive, complex tangent, and real tangent dimensions. The binary code dimension $k=12$ is a separate result of Definition Z.13b.0 and Theorem Z.13b.0a, as recorded in Corollary Z.2.5c. ∎

**Corollary Z.2.5c (Projector-Dimension Ledger and Code-Dimension Separation).** Assume the minimal Hilbert-carrier branch $E=\mathbb C^8$ and the rank-two active-projector branch. Then the projector $p$ determines
$$
b=\dim_{\mathbb C}(1-p)E=6,
\qquad
q_{\mathrm{geom}}:=\dim_{\mathbb C}T_p\operatorname{Gr}(2,8)=12,
\qquad
M:=\dim_{\mathbb R}T_p\operatorname{Gr}(2,8)=24.
$$
The binary code dimension $k=12$ is a separate conclusion of Definition Z.13b.0 and Theorem Z.13b.0a. Identification with the extended Golay code further requires Theorem Z.13b's code-class and maximum-distance hypotheses or an accepted Type-II parent-code certificate of Definition Z.13b.P0.

*Proof.* Since $\operatorname{rank}_{\mathbb C}p=2$ on $E=\mathbb C^8$, the complement has dimension $8-2=6$. The tangent space is $\operatorname{Hom}(pE,(1-p)E)$, so its complex dimension is $2\cdot6=12$ and its real dimension is $2\cdot12=24$. A binary code $C\subset\mathbb F_2^{24}$ is not part of the projector data. On the predictive-recovery MacWilliams branch, Theorem Z.13b.0a separately gives $\dim_{\mathbb F_2}C=24/2=12$. ∎

**Corollary Z.2.5d (Peirce-Steiner Electroweak Ledger).** Let $B$ be the pair-octad incidence matrix of the Witt design $S(5,8,24)$ and let
$$
\mathcal N_{\mathrm{GS}}=B^TB.
$$
If the active Peirce pair of $p$ is represented by an unordered pair $p_A$ in the $24$-mode Witt ledger, then
$$
\langle e_{p_A},\mathcal N_{\mathrm{GS}}e_{p_A}\rangle
=
77.
\tag{Z.2.5d.1}
$$
Consequently, on the active-pair electroweak branch of Appendix T,
$$
2\kappa_{EW}=77.
\tag{Z.2.5d.2}
$$

*Proof.* The diagonal entry
$$
\langle e_{p_A},B^TBe_{p_A}\rangle
=
\sum_{O\in S(5,8,24)}B_{O,p_A}^2
$$
counts octads containing the fixed pair $p_A$. In a Steiner system $S(5,8,24)$, the number of blocks containing a fixed pair is
$$
\lambda_2
=
\frac{\binom{24-2}{5-2}}{\binom{8-2}{5-2}}
=
\frac{\binom{22}{3}}{\binom{6}{3}}
=
77.
$$
Definition T.13 uses this pair-incidence trace as the registered fixed-time quadratic response action, and Theorem T.5 gives $2\kappa_{EW}=77$. Gaussian zero modes belong to the separate prefactor ledger. ∎

## Z.3 Conditional Carrier and Active-Kernel Consistency

### Z.3.1 The SPAP Cycle Architecture

**Definition Z.1 (SPAP Register Structure).** On Theorem 15's realization class satisfying (O1)–(O3) and (FC), the registered SPAP encoding uses three functionally distinct, simultaneously readable binary coordinates:

| Register | Symbol | Role | Cardinality |
|----------|--------|------|-------------|
| State | $\phi$ | System state component under reflexive update | $\{0,1\}$ |
| Prediction | $p_{\mathrm{stored}}$ | Stored prediction for verification | $\{0,1\}$ |
| Phase | $c_{\mathrm{phase}}$ | Cycle control (generate vs. update phase) | $\{0,1\}$ |

The SPAP update rule is $\phi_{t+1}=\operatorname{NOT}(p_{\mathrm{stored}})$, where $p_{\mathrm{stored}}$ is retained across the prepare/predict/store phase for use at the reflexive update.

**Lemma Z.1 (Minimal Configuration Count on the Full-Context Branch).** Let $\mathcal S_{\mathrm{vis}}$ be the finite visited-context set of a realization satisfying (O1)–(O3) and (FC). Then the joint readout

$$
r=(r_m,r_p,r_v):\mathcal S_{\mathrm{vis}}\longrightarrow\{0,1\}^3,
\qquad
r(s)=\bigl(r_m(s),r_p(s),r_v(s)\bigr),
$$

is surjective, and therefore $|\mathcal S_{\mathrm{vis}}|\ge8$.

*Proof.* By (FC), every element of $\{0,1\}^3$ has a preimage under $r$. Hence

$$
|\mathcal S_{\mathrm{vis}}|
\ge
|\{0,1\}^3|
=8.
$$

This is a consequence of the full-context surjection; (O1) alone does not impose the eight-context count. ∎
### Z.3.2 The Irreversible Merge Structure

**Lemma Z.2 (Conditional Merge in the Declared Binary-Ancilla Architecture).** Assume all four pairs $(\phi,p)\in\{0,1\}^2$ are reachable immediately before reset and the accessible completed-cycle output fixes $p=p_{\mathrm{ready}}$. Then the accessible cycle map is noninjective and at least one output has two or more preimages. SPAP alone does not establish the full-support or fixed-ready-state hypotheses.

*Proof.* The stated domain has four elements and the fixed-ready-state codomain has two. The pigeonhole principle gives a fiber of size at least two. If the full reachable set is smaller, or an injective extension retains the displaced label, this conclusion need not follow. ∎

**Lemma Z.3 (Conditional Landauer Cost of the Declared Reset).** Under Lemma Z.2, let $P$ be the reset record, $R$ all retained classical side information, and $q(P,R)$ their actual pre-reset law. On the registered isothermal-reset branch,
$$
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{reset}}\ge H_q(P\mid R).
$$
If $H_q(P\mid R)=\ln2$, then $\varepsilon_{\mathrm{reset}}\ge\ln2$; equality additionally requires zero excess dissipation. The structural alphabet value $\varepsilon_0=\ln2$ does not by itself impose this physical lower bound. ∎

### Z.3.3 The Structural Constraint
**Theorem Z.2 (Conditional Minimal-Carrier and Active-Kernel Consistency).** Assume the PCE-minimal active operational branch carries Theorem 15's (O1)–(O3), (FC) SPAP-register realization and represents its visited contexts by mutually perfectly distinguishable Hilbert alternatives. Assume also that every admissible candidate on this branch with $d_0>8$ has an admissible eight-dimensional comparator in the same optimization class with the identical registered protocol-response family and identical non-support benefit, penalty, and propagation ledger entries, but strictly lower maintained-support cost. Finally assume the active-kernel branch of Theorem Z.1, including its sharp binary verification record and no-surplus hypotheses, so that $a=2$. Then
$$
d_0=8=2a^2.
$$

*Proof.*

**Step 1 (Lower bound).** On the assumed (O1)–(O3), (FC) branch, Theorem 15 gives $N_{\mathrm{vis}}^{\min}=2^{K_0}=8$. Mutual perfect distinguishability of the context representatives and Theorem 23 give $d_0\ge N_{\mathrm{vis}}^{\min}=8$.

**Step 2 (Registered comparator).** The comparator hypothesis supplies an admissible eight-dimensional realization with the same registered response and strictly smaller maintained-support cost than every candidate with $d_0>8$. Hence no larger candidate minimizes the total PCE potential, and $d_0\le8$. Together with Step 1, $d_0=8$. This argument invokes the registered comparator directly; it does not assert a universal tensor-factor decomposition or universal quotientability of every larger carrier.

**Step 3 (Active-kernel identity).** On the separately assumed Theorem-Z.1 branch, $a=2$, so $2a^2=2\cdot4=8=d_0$. This final equality is a branch consistency identity, not an independent SPAP-only derivation of $a$. ∎

**Corollary Z.1 (Consistency Identities on the Joint Minimal Branch).** On the joint branch of Theorem Z.2 and Theorem Z.1,
$$
(d_0,a,K_0,\varepsilon_0)=(8,2,3,\ln2).
$$
Consequently,
$$
K_0=\log_2d_0=3=1+2\log_2a,
\qquad
\frac{a}{d_0}=\frac14,
\qquad
\frac{b}{d_0}=\frac34.
$$
If the additional active-entropy saturation $\varepsilon_0=\ln a$ is imposed, the numerical identity $K_0=1+2\varepsilon_0/\ln2=3$ also holds. These are consistency identities at the selected point; they do not define a law $d_0=2a^2$ or $K_0=1+2\varepsilon/\ln2$ for counterfactual values of $a$ or $\varepsilon$.

## Z.4 Algebraic Constraints on d_0

### Z.4.1 Division Algebra Structure

**Theorem Z.3 (Hurwitz Classification of Real Normed Division Algebras).** Let $A$ be a finite-dimensional unital algebra over $\mathbb R$ equipped with a positive-definite norm $N$ satisfying
$$
N(xy)=N(x)N(y)
$$
for all $x,y\in A$, and suppose every nonzero element of $A$ is invertible. Then
$$
\dim_{\mathbb R}A\in\{1,2,4,8\},
$$
and $A$ is isomorphic as a normed algebra to $\mathbb R$, $\mathbb C$, $\mathbb H$, or $\mathbb O$, respectively.

*Proof.* The hypotheses are exactly those of Hurwitz's classification theorem for finite-dimensional real normed division algebras (Hurwitz 1898). That theorem states that every such algebra is one of $\mathbb R$, $\mathbb C$, $\mathbb H$, or $\mathbb O$. Their real dimensions are $1,2,4,8$, respectively. The conclusion does not extend to real division algebras without the multiplicative-norm hypothesis. ∎

### Z.4.2 Uniqueness of d_0 = 8

**Corollary Z.2 (Minimal-Branch Determination of $d_0 = 8$).** On the minimal PCE branch, the Hilbert-space dimension is uniquely
$$
d_0 = 8.
$$

*Proof.* Theorem 15 fixes the finite operational-context floor
$$
N_{\mathrm{vis}}^{\min}=2^{K_0}=8.
$$
On the Hilbert-carrier branch, representing these contexts as mutually perfectly distinguishable alternatives gives the exact lower bound
$$
d_0 \ge N_{\mathrm{vis}}^{\min}=8.
$$
Under Theorem Z.2's explicit same-class eight-state comparator, response-preserving quotientability, and strictly increasing maintained-support cost, every candidate with $d_0>8$ is dominated by an admissible $d_0=8$ representative. Hence
$$
d_0=8
$$
on that branch. Without those hypotheses only $d_0\ge8$ follows.

Hurwitz's classification theorem (Theorem Z.3) supplies a secondary algebraic coherence check: $8$ is the largest dimension in the finite list of real normed division algebras. This numerical context is not part of the $d_0=8$ necessity proof. ∎

**Remark Z.1 (Octonionic Dimensional Comparison).** The equality $d_0=8$ matches the real dimension of the octonions. This numerical match does not identify the complex Hilbert carrier $\mathcal H_0\cong\mathbb C^8$ with $\mathbb O$, construct an octonionic multiplication on $\mathcal H_0$, or derive an $E_8$ or lattice embedding; each such bridge requires separately specified structure.

**Remark Z.1a: Algebraic Context of $d_0 = 8$.** The MPU Hilbert space dimension $d_0 = 8$ on the minimal PCE branch (Theorem Z.2), with Theorem 23 supplying the lower bound $d_0 \ge 8$ for $K_0 = 3$ (Theorem 15), occupies a distinguished position in algebraic structure.

*Division Algebra Correspondence.* Hurwitz's classification theorem (Theorem Z.3) establishes that finite-dimensional real unital normed division algebras with multiplicative positive-definite norm are $\mathbb R$, $\mathbb C$, $\mathbb H$, and $\mathbb O$, of dimensions $1,2,4,8$. The value $d_0 = 8$ coincides with $\dim_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathbb{O})$, placing the MPU state space at the maximal division algebra dimension.

*Clifford Algebra Structure.* The $K_0 = 3$ qubit workspace supports six pairwise anti-commuting operators
$$\gamma_{2k-1} = \left(\prod_{j=1}^{k-1}\sigma_z^{(j)}\right)\sigma_x^{(k)}, \qquad \gamma_{2k} = \left(\prod_{j=1}^{k-1}\sigma_z^{(j)}\right)\sigma_y^{(k)}, \qquad k=1,2,3$$
which satisfy the Clifford relations $\{\gamma_i,\gamma_j\} = 2\delta_{ij} I$ and generate $\text{Cl}_{\mathbb{C}}(6) \cong M_8(\mathbb{C})$. The algebra dimension $2^6 = 64 = d_0^2$ matches the operator algebra $\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_0)$.

*Bott Periodicity.* Real and complex Clifford algebras exhibit 8-fold and 2-fold periodicity respectively. The value $d_0 = 8 = 2^3$ sits at the end of the first real period, where algebraic structure is maximally rich before repeating.

*Clifford Complexification Bridge.* The standard complexification identity $\mathrm{Cl}_{\mathbb{R}}(0,n)\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{C}\cong\mathrm{Cl}_{\mathbb{C}}(n)$ at $n=6$ gives
$$
\mathrm{Cl}_{\mathbb{C}}(6)\cong\mathrm{Cl}_{\mathbb{R}}(0,6)\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{C}.
$$
From the Bott periodicity table, $\mathrm{Cl}_{\mathbb{R}}(0,6)\cong M_8(\mathbb{R})$. Thus the minimal SPAP algebra
$$
\mathfrak{A}_{\min}\cong M_8(\mathbb{C})\cong\mathrm{Cl}_{\mathbb{C}}(6)\cong\mathrm{Cl}_{\mathbb{R}}(0,6)\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{C}
$$
is the complexification of the $64$-dimensional real associative algebra $M_8(\mathbb{R})$, which acts faithfully on the eight-dimensional real module $\mathbb{R}^8$. The octonions $\mathbb O$ form an eight-dimensional real nonassociative algebra. The two constructions share the numeral eight only as, respectively, the Clifford-module dimension and the octonion-algebra dimension; no algebra isomorphism or common algebraic foundation follows from that equality.

The equality $d_0=8$ follows on Theorem Z.2's (O1)–(O3), (FC), Hilbert-distinguishability, and same-class-comparator branch. The identity $d_0=2a^2$ is recorded only after the separate Theorem-Z.1 branch fixes $a=2$.

Theorem Z.11 proves that the registered value $M=24$ selects the least feasible dimension $D=4$ inside the faithful Bures tangent-shell contract: $K(3)=12$ excludes lower dimensions and the regular $24$-cell supplies the required realization in four. The current theorem package does not assign $d_0$, $a$, or $M$ to counterfactual values $K_0\ne3$; each such branch would require its own carrier, active-kernel, faithful-shell, and least-cost certificates.

**Definition Z.3a (Radon-Hurwitz/Golay Compatibility Data).** On the minimal branch $d_0=8$, let
$$
\gamma_{2k-1}
=
\left(\prod_{j=1}^{k-1}\sigma_z^{(j)}\right)\sigma_x^{(k)},
\qquad
\gamma_{2k}
=
\left(\prod_{j=1}^{k-1}\sigma_z^{(j)}\right)\sigma_y^{(k)},
\qquad
k=1,2,3,
$$
be the six Clifford generators of Remark Z.1a. Define
$$
E_\alpha=i\gamma_\alpha\in\mathfrak{su}(8),
\qquad
\alpha=1,\dots,6.
\tag{Z.3a.1}
$$
For a retained Peter-Weyl threshold block
$$
b=(\Lambda,s,a),
$$
let $V_\Lambda$ be the irreducible $SU(8)$ module of highest weight $\Lambda$, let $M_{\Lambda,s,a}$ be the finite multiplicity space
$$
M_{\Lambda,s,a}
=
\operatorname{Hom}_H(\tau_{s,a},V_\Lambda|_H),
$$
and set
$$
\mathcal H_b=V_\Lambda\otimes M_{\Lambda,s,a}.
$$
The functorial RH lift to $b$ is
$$
A_{\alpha,b}
=
d\rho_\Lambda(E_\alpha)\otimes I_{M_{\Lambda,s,a}}.
\tag{Z.3a.2}
$$
Let $\Pi_{\mathrm{syn},b}$ be the transported marked Golay syndrome projector, let $J_{0,b}$ be the transported marked Golay half-swap, and let $\Pi_{r,b}$ be the lifted sector projection on the same finite block. The RHG compatibility operator is
$$
\mathcal K_b
=
\sum_{\alpha=1}^{6}
[A_{\alpha,b},\Pi_{\mathrm{syn},b}]^*
[A_{\alpha,b},\Pi_{\mathrm{syn},b}]
+
\sum_{\alpha=1}^{6}
[A_{\alpha,b},J_{0,b}]^*
[A_{\alpha,b},J_{0,b}]
+
\sum_r\sum_{\alpha=1}^{6}
[A_{\alpha,b},\Pi_{r,b}]^*
[A_{\alpha,b},\Pi_{r,b}].
\tag{Z.3a.3}
$$
The RHG projection is
$$
\Pi_b^{\mathrm{RHG}}
=
\mathbf 1_{\{\lambda_{\min}(\mathcal K_b)\}}(\mathcal K_b).
\tag{Z.3a.4}
$$

**Theorem Z.3a.1 (Radon-Hurwitz/Golay Spectral Selection).** For every finite retained threshold block $b$, the projection $\Pi_b^{\mathrm{RHG}}$ is a nonzero orthogonal projector determined by the minimal $d_0=8$ Clifford real form, the marked Golay syndrome data, the marked half-swap, and the lifted sector projections. It is invariant under data-preserving unitary changes of basis. If the exact RH/Golay compatibility equations have a common solution, $\Pi_b^{\mathrm{RHG}}$ is the exact compatible projection. Otherwise it is the finite PCE-minimal least-defect projection.

*Proof.* Equation (Z.3a.2) is functorial under equivalent presentations of $V_\Lambda$. The marked Golay data and sector projections are fixed finite matrices once the marked branch is fixed. Each summand in (Z.3a.3) has the form $B^*B$, so $\mathcal K_b$ is positive self-adjoint on a finite-dimensional space. Therefore it has a lowest eigenvalue and a nonzero orthogonal spectral projection.

Data-preserving unitary changes of basis conjugate all matrices in (Z.3a.3), so they conjugate $\mathcal K_b$ and preserve its lowest eigenspace. If the common compatibility equations are solvable, the simultaneous kernel of all commutators is nonzero and equals $\ker\mathcal K_b$. Then the lowest eigenvalue is $0$ and $\Pi_b^{\mathrm{RHG}}$ is the exact compatible projection. If the common kernel is zero, the Rayleigh quotient of $\mathcal K_b$ is the total squared compatibility defect, and the lowest spectral subspace is the uniquely determined eigenspace on which that quotient attains its minimum. Calling this subspace PCE-selected additionally requires a registered comparison class in which the protocol role and every non-defect ledger entry are fixed and the defect term is strictly ordered. ∎

---

## Z.5 Modular Forms and the Ordered $(12,24)$ Ledger

### Z.5.1 The Dedekind Eta Function and the Ramanujan Discriminant

**Theorem Z.4 (Ramanujan Discriminant Ledger).** In the normalized Ramanujan discriminant convention, the discriminant sector carries the ordered ledger
$$
(\operatorname{weight}(\Delta),\operatorname{exp}_{\eta}(\Delta))=(12,24).
$$

The Dedekind eta function is
$$
\eta(\tau)=q^{1/24}\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}(1-q^n),
\qquad
q=e^{2\pi i\tau}.
$$
Therefore
$$
\eta(\tau)^{24}
=
q\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}(1-q^n)^{24}.
$$
In the normalized modular-form convention,
$$
\Delta(\tau)
=
q\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}(1-q^n)^{24}
=
\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\tau(n)q^n,
$$
where $\tau(n)$ is Ramanujan's tau function. The modular discriminant is the level-$1$ weight-$12$ cusp form generated by the eta product
$$
\Delta(\tau)=\eta(\tau)^{24}.
$$
Some analytic conventions include a global factor $(2\pi)^{12}$ in the Weierstrass-discriminant normalization. That factor changes neither the eta exponent, nor the modular weight, nor the Fourier-coefficient ledger used here.

*Proof.* The eta function has modular weight $1/2$ with multiplier system. Taking the twenty-fourth power removes the multiplier and gives weight $24\cdot\frac12=12$. The product formula gives the Fourier expansion above, whose coefficients define Ramanujan's tau function. Hence the discriminant sector carries eta exponent $24$ and modular weight $12$. ∎

### Z.5.2 The PU Minimal Interface Ledger

On the minimal PU active-kernel branch,
$$
K_0=3,
\qquad
 d_0=8,
\qquad
 a=2,
\qquad
 b=d_0-a=6.
$$
The PCE-Attractor state is
$$
\rho_0=\frac12 I_2\oplus0_6.
$$
Its unitary orbit is
$$
\mathcal O_{\rho_0}
=
\{U\rho_0U^\dagger:U\in U(8)\}
\cong
U(8)/(U(2)\times U(6))
\cong
\mathrm{Gr}(2,8).
$$

**Theorem Z.5.1a (PU Minimal Ordered Interface Ledger).** On the minimal active-kernel branch, the PU interface ledger is
$$
\mathfrak L_{\mathrm{PU}}=(k,M)=(12,24),
$$
where
$$
k=\dim_{\mathbb C}\mathrm{Gr}(2,8)=ab=12
$$
and
$$
M=\dim_{\mathbb R}\mathrm{Gr}(2,8)=2ab=24.
$$

*Proof.* The complex Grassmannian $\mathrm{Gr}(a,d_0)$ has complex dimension $a(d_0-a)$. Substituting $a=2$ and $d_0=8$ gives
$$
\dim_{\mathbb C}\mathrm{Gr}(2,8)=2(8-2)=12.
$$
A complex manifold of complex dimension $12$ has real dimension $24$. Equivalently,
$$
\dim_{\mathbb R}U(8)-\dim_{\mathbb R}U(2)-\dim_{\mathbb R}U(6)
=
64-4-36
=
24.
$$
By Theorem Z.5, the QFI-active interface count on the same branch is $M=2ab=24$. Thus the Grassmannian tangent count and the QFI interface-mode count agree on the minimal branch. ∎

### Z.5.3 Exact Ramanujan-PU Ledger Bridge

Define the Ramanujan discriminant ledger
$$
\mathfrak L_{\Delta}
=
(\operatorname{weight}(\Delta),\operatorname{exp}_{\eta}(\Delta))
=
(12,24),
$$
where $\operatorname{exp}_{\eta}(\Delta)$ is the exponent in $\Delta=\eta^{24}$.

**Theorem Z.5.2a (Exact Ordered $(12,24)$ Ledger Bridge).** On the PU minimal active-kernel branch and in the normalized Ramanujan discriminant sector,
$$
k
=
\dim_{\mathbb C}\mathrm{Gr}(2,8)
=
\operatorname{weight}(\Delta)
=
12
$$
and
$$
M
=
\dim_{\mathbb R}\mathrm{Gr}(2,8)
=
\operatorname{exp}_{\eta}(\Delta)
=
24.
$$
Therefore
$$
\mathfrak L_{\mathrm{PU}}
=
(12,24)
=
\mathfrak L_{\Delta}.
$$

*Proof.* The PU side is Theorem Z.5.1a. The Ramanujan side is Theorem Z.4. Componentwise comparison gives equality of the ordered ledger. No claim of a canonical isomorphism between the Grassmannian and the modular-form space is used. ∎

**Corollary Z.5.2a.1 (No-Numerology Criterion).** A proposed bridge to the PU interface sector is admissible at this ledger level only if it preserves the ordered pair $(12,24)$ and the doubling relation
$$
24=2\cdot12.
$$
On the PU side the doubling is
$$
\dim_{\mathbb R}=2\dim_{\mathbb C}.
$$
On the Ramanujan side the doubling is
$$
\eta\text{-exponent}=2\cdot\operatorname{weight}.
$$
A bare occurrence of $24$ without the paired weight-$12$ entry does not satisfy this criterion.

### Z.5.4 Leech Closure of the Discriminant Bridge

Let $\Lambda_{24}$ denote the Leech lattice. In the normalized modular convention its theta series satisfies
$$
\Theta_{\Lambda_{24}}(\tau)=E_4(\tau)^3-720\Delta(\tau).
$$

**Theorem Z.5.3a (Leech-Discriminant Correction).** The Ramanujan discriminant is the weight-$12$ modular correction that removes the norm-$2$ root shell from the $24$-dimensional even-unimodular theta sector, yielding the rootless Leech lattice theta series.

*Proof.* The Eisenstein series $E_4$ has weight $4$, so $E_4^3$ has weight $12$. The discriminant $\Delta$ also has weight $12$. Hence $E_4^3-720\Delta$ is a weight-$12$ modular form. In normalized $q$-coordinates,
$$
E_4(\tau)=1+240q+O(q^2),
\qquad
\Delta(\tau)=q+O(q^2),
$$
so
$$
E_4(\tau)^3-720\Delta(\tau)=1+O(q^2).
$$
Thus the $q^1$ term, which records norm-$2$ roots in the even-unimodular rank-$24$ theta convention, is cancelled. The unique even-unimodular rank-$24$ rootless lattice is the Leech lattice, so the resulting rootless rank-$24$ theta series is $\Theta_{\Lambda_{24}}$. ∎

**Corollary Z.5.3a.1 (Three-Sector Ordered Ledger).** The same ordered ledger appears as PU interface geometry, Ramanujan modular arithmetic, and Leech lattice structure:
$$
\begin{array}{c|c|c}
\text{PU} & \text{Ramanujan} & \text{Leech}\\
\hline
\mathrm{Gr}(2,8) & \Delta=\eta^{24} & \Lambda_{24}\\
\dim_{\mathbb C}=12 & \operatorname{weight}(\Delta)=12 & \operatorname{weight}(\Theta_{\Lambda_{24}})=12\\
\dim_{\mathbb R}=24 & \eta\text{-exponent}=24 & \operatorname{rank}(\Lambda_{24})=24
\end{array}
$$
Thus
$$
\mathrm{Gr}(2,8)
\quad\longleftrightarrow\quad
\Delta=\eta^{24}
\quad\longleftrightarrow\quad
\Lambda_{24}
$$
is an ordered $(12,24)$ ledger bridge, not an isolated coincidence of the number $24$.

### Z.5.5 Relation to the Fine-Structure Branch

**Remark Z.5.4a (Scope of the Modular Bridge).** The ordered $(12,24)$ bridge records an exact structural compatibility among the PU interface ledger, the Ramanujan discriminant ledger, and the Leech theta ledger. It does not by itself derive the electromagnetic coupling, the Thomson-limit normalization, or the Appendix Z numerical value of $\alpha^{-1}$. Those conclusions still require the later capacity-saturation, Ward-normalization, interface-correction, and residual-certificate branches. The bridge supplies a ledger-level structural cross-check for the same minimal $M=24$, $k=12$ backbone.

# PART II: INFORMATION STRUCTURE (Sections Z.6–Z.8)

## Z.6 Explicit Construction of the PCE-Attractor State

### Z.6.1 The Computational Basis

**Definition Z.2 (Computational Basis).** In the computational basis $\{|b_2b_1b_0\rangle:b_j\in\{0,1\}\}$ of the 3-qubit system $\mathcal H_0=(\mathbb C^2)^{\otimes 3}$, the eight basis states are:
$$|000\rangle, |001\rangle, |010\rangle, |011\rangle, |100\rangle, |101\rangle, |110\rangle, |111\rangle$$

### Z.6.2 Uniqueness of the Active Subspace

**Proposition Z.1 (Uniqueness of Active Subspace).** Any choice of an $a=2$ dimensional subspace of $\mathcal H_0=\mathbb C^8$ yields the same QFI mode structure, up to unitary equivalence. The algebraic and metric properties of the $M=24$ interface modes do not depend on which 2-dimensional subspace is designated "active."

*Proof.* The group $SU(8)$ acts transitively on the Grassmannian $\operatorname{Gr}(2,8)$ of 2-dimensional subspaces. Any two choices of active subspace are related by a unitary transformation $U\in SU(8)$. Since the QFI is unitarily invariant,
$$
F_Q[U\rho U^\dagger,UGU^\dagger]=F_Q[\rho,G],
$$
the mode spectrum is independent of the specific choice of active subspace. ∎

### Z.6.3 The PCE-Attractor State

**Definition Z.3 (PCE-Attractor State).** The canonical realization of the PCE-Attractor selects the computational basis states $|000\rangle$ and $|111\rangle$ as the active subspace:
$$\rho_0 = \frac{1}{2}(|000\rangle\langle 000| + |111\rangle\langle 111|)$$

This choice has maximal symmetry under the permutation group $S_3$ acting on qubit indices.

**Theorem Z.6.3a (PCE-Attractor Orbit Structure).** The orbit of the PCE-Attractor state $\rho_0 = \frac{1}{a}I_a \oplus 0_b$ under unitary conjugation is:
$$\mathcal{O}_{\rho_0} \cong U(d_0)/(U(a) \times U(b)) \cong \operatorname{Gr}(a, d_0) = \operatorname{Gr}(2,8)$$
a compact Hermitian symmetric space with:
- Complex dimension: $\dim_{\mathbb{C}} = ab = 12$
- Real dimension: $\dim_{\mathbb{R}} = 2ab = 24 = M$

*Proof.* The isotropy group of $\rho_0$ under $U(d_0)$ conjugation is $U(a) \times U(b)$ (block-diagonal unitaries preserving the active/inactive partition). The quotient $U(d_0)/(U(a) \times U(b))$ is the Grassmannian of $a$-planes in $\mathbb{C}^{d_0}$. $\square$

**Remark Z.6.3b: Geometric Interpretation of Mode Count.** The equality $\dim_{\mathbb{R}}(\operatorname{Gr}(2,8)) = M$ is not coincidental: the QFI-active interface modes (Theorem Z.5) parametrize the tangent space to the attractor orbit. This provides an independent geometric derivation of the mode count $M = 24$.

**Proposition Z.6.3c (Kähler Moment-Map Normal Form and Stability Boundary).** Let
$$
X=\mathrm{Gr}(a,d_0)
$$
be the attractor orbit of Theorem Z.6.3a, represented by rank-$a$ orthogonal projectors $P$ on $\mathbb C^{d_0}$. With the standard $SU(d_0)$-invariant Kähler form on $X$, the moment map for the $SU(d_0)$ action is
$$
\mu(P)=i\left(P-\frac{a}{d_0}I_{d_0}\right)
\in\mathfrak{su}(d_0)^*,
$$
after identifying $\mathfrak{su}(d_0)^*$ with traceless Hermitian matrices by the trace pairing. Its squared norm is constant on the orbit:
$$
\|\mu(P)\|^2
=
\operatorname{Tr}\left(P-\frac{a}{d_0}I_{d_0}\right)^2
=
\frac{a(d_0-a)}{d_0}.
$$
On the PU branch $a=2$, $d_0=8$,
$$
\|\mu(P)\|^2=\frac{2\cdot6}{8}=\frac32.
$$
Therefore an $SU(8)$-invariant PCE functional depending only on $\|\mu\|^2$ cannot select a point, a preferred internal flag, or the ordered $(3,2,1)$ gauge splitting inside $\mathrm{Gr}(2,8)$. The Kähler moment-map statement supplies the orbit normal form and the symplectic/QFI tangent structure. The ordered gauge-sector splitting requires symmetry reduction to the flag lift of Theorem G.8.4e.1.

*Proof.* The Grassmannian is the coadjoint orbit of a rank-$a$ projector under $SU(d_0)$. The trace of $P$ is $a$, so the traceless Hermitian representative of the moment map is
$$
P-\frac{a}{d_0}I_{d_0}.
$$
Since $P^2=P$ and $\operatorname{Tr}P=a$,
$$
\operatorname{Tr}\left(P-\frac{a}{d_0}I_{d_0}\right)^2
=
\operatorname{Tr}(P^2)
-
2\frac{a}{d_0}\operatorname{Tr}P
+
\frac{a^2}{d_0^2}\operatorname{Tr}I_{d_0}.
$$
Substitution gives
$$
a-2\frac{a^2}{d_0}+\frac{a^2}{d_0}
=
a-\frac{a^2}{d_0}
=
\frac{a(d_0-a)}{d_0}.
$$
This depends only on $a$ and $d_0$, not on the point $P\in X$. Hence any $SU(d_0)$-invariant functional of $\|\mu\|^2$ alone is constant on $X$ and cannot select a point or ordered subbundle. The remaining statements follow from Theorem Z.5, Theorem Z.6.3a, and Theorem G.8.4e.1. ∎

## Z.7 QFI Spectrum at the PCE-Attractor

### Z.7.1 Generator Classification

The $64$ generators of the $8\times8$ density-matrix space decompose under the active/inactive partition:

| Block | Dimension | Generators | QFI Contribution |
|-------|-----------|------------|------------------|
| Active-Active (AA) | $a^2=4$ | $\{E_{jk}\}_{j,k\in A}$ | $F_Q=0$ (intra-subspace) |
| Inactive-Inactive (BB) | $b^2=36$ | $\{E_{jk}\}_{j,k\in B}$ | $F_Q=0$ (zero eigenvalues) |
| Active-Inactive (AB) | $ab=12$ | $\{E_{jk}\}_{j\in A,\,k\in B}$ | $F_Q>0$ (interface) |
| Inactive-Active (BA) | $ab=12$ | $\{E_{jk}\}_{j\in B,\,k\in A}$ | $F_Q>0$ (interface) |

### Z.7.2 QFI Mode Count

**Theorem Z.5 (QFI Mode Count).** At the PCE-Attractor $\rho_0=I_a/a\oplus0_b$, the number of linearly independent QFI-active generators is:
$$M = 2ab = 2 \times 2 \times 6 = 24$$

Each active generator has the same QFI eigenvalue $\lambda=1$.

*Proof.* Consider a traceless Hermitian generator $G$ with matrix elements $G_{jk}$.

**Step 1: QFI Formula.** The quantum Fisher information for a state $\rho$ with spectral decomposition $\rho=\sum_i p_i|i\rangle\langle i|$ is:
$$F_Q[\rho, G] = 2\sum_{i,j: p_i + p_j > 0} \frac{(p_i - p_j)^2}{p_i + p_j}|\langle i|G|j\rangle|^2$$

**Step 2: Eigenvalue Structure.** For $\rho_0=I_a/a\oplus0_b$:
- Active indices $A=\{1,2\}$: eigenvalue $p_j=1/a=1/2$
- Inactive indices $B=\{3,\ldots,8\}$: eigenvalue $p_k=0$

**Step 3: Block Analysis.**

*AA block ($j,k\in A$):* Both $p_j=p_k=1/a$, so $(p_j-p_k)^2=0$. No contribution to $F_Q$.

*BB block ($j,k\in B$):* Both $p_j=p_k=0$, so $p_j+p_k=0$. This term is excluded from the sum.

*AB block ($j\in A$, $k\in B$):* $p_j=1/a$ and $p_k=0$, so:
$$\frac{(p_j - p_k)^2}{p_j + p_k} = \frac{(1/a)^2}{1/a} = \frac{1}{a}$$

*BA block:* Same as AB by Hermiticity.

**Step 4: Interface Mode Counting.** Each $(j,k)$ pair with $j\in A$ and $k\in B$ contributes one complex degree of freedom to the generator space. There are $ab=2\times6=12$ such pairs. Including both the symmetric ($S_{jk}$) and antisymmetric ($A_{jk}$) combinations, the total number of real interface generators is $2ab=24$.

**Step 5: QFI Eigenvalue.** For a Hilbert–Schmidt-normalized interface generator $G$ with
$$
\lVert G\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}^2=\sum_{j,k}|G_{jk}|^2=1,
$$
the QFI is
$$F_Q[\rho_0, G] = 2 \times \frac{1}{a} \times 1 = \frac{2}{a} = 1$$
for $a=2$. Each interface generator has QFI eigenvalue $\lambda=1$.

**Step 6: Isotropy and Flatness.** The flatness needed here follows directly from the SLD-QFI formula above, not from transitivity on the full interface unit sphere. For an arbitrary Hilbert–Schmidt-normalized interface generator $G\in AB\oplus BA$, the only nonzero SLD-QFI contributions are the active-inactive matrix entries, and Step 3 gives the same coefficient $1/a$ for each such entry. Hence Step 5 gives $F_Q[\rho_0,G]=2/a$ for every normalized interface generator. With $a=2$, every real interface basis direction has QFI eigenvalue $\lambda=1$, and in the normalized real interface basis the restricted QFI metric is $g_{\mathrm{QFI}}=I_{24}$. ∎

### Z.7.3 Factor Structure of M = 24

**Remark Z.3: Three Factors of M = 24.** The mode count factorizes as:
$$M = 2 \times a \times b = 2 \times 2 \times 6 = 24$$

Each factor has independent origin:
- Factor 2: Complex structure (Hermitian = symmetric + antisymmetric)
- Factor $a=2$: active subspace dimension from the sharp binary record, structural $\varepsilon_0=\ln2$ capacity gate, and no-surplus selection (Theorem Z.1)


- Factor $b = 6$: Inactive subspace dimension (from $d_0 - a = 8 - 2$)

### Z.7.4 QFI Complexity Functional

**Definition Z.7.4a (QFI Complexity Functional).** The complexity of a state $\rho$ relative to the interface structure is:

$$\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{QFI}}[\rho] := \frac{1}{M} \sum_{\mu=1}^{M} F_Q[\rho; G_\mu]$$

where $G_\mu$ are the $M = 2ab = 24$ interface generators (Theorem Z.5). This measure equals the average QFI across information-carrying modes. At the PCE-Attractor:

$$\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{QFI}}[\rho_0] = \frac{1}{24} \times 24 \times 1 = 1$$

since each interface generator has $F_Q[\rho_0; G_\mu] = 1$ (Theorem Z.5, Step 5).

**Properties:**
- $\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{QFI}}[\rho] \geq 0$ (non-negative, since QFI $\geq 0$)
- $\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{QFI}}[\rho] = 0$ iff $[\rho, G_\mu] = 0$ for all $\mu$
- Continuous in $\rho$ for full-rank states

---

## Z.8 Capacity Saturation and the Bare Coupling

### Z.8.1 The Operational Alphabet-Capacity Theorem

**Definition Z.4 (Operational Alphabet Capacity).** The operational alphabet capacity of a state $\rho$ at coupling strength $u$ is quantified by the LAN/QFI capacity surrogate $g_{\rm true}(u)$ (Appendix W):
$$
g_{\rm true}(u)=\sum_{i=1}^M \ln(1+\lambda_i u),
$$
where $\{\lambda_i\}$ are the QFI eigenvalues of the propagated channel linearized at $u=0$.

**Theorem Z.6 (Operational Alphabet-Capacity).** For the PCE-Attractor state, the operational alphabet capacity is:
$$g_{\rm true}(u)=\sum_{i=1}^M \ln(1+\lambda_i u)=24\ln(1+u),$$
where $u$ is the coupling strength and $\lambda_i=1$ is the (flat) QFI spectrum on the interface.

*Proof.* The LAN/QFI capacity surrogate used throughout PU (Appendix W, Eq. (W.0.1)) is
$$
g_{\rm true}(u)=\sum_{i=1}^M \ln(1+\lambda_i u).
$$
By Theorem Z.5, the QFI spectrum is flat at the attractor: $\lambda_i=1$ for all $i$. Therefore
$$
g_{\rm true}(u)=\sum_{i=1}^M \ln(1+u)=M\ln(1+u)=24\ln(1+u).
$$
∎

### Z.8.2 The Bare Coupling

**Theorem Z.7 (Bare Rate Coordinate on the Cap-Active LAN/QFI-Surrogate Branch).** Assume that the LAN/QFI surrogate
$$
g_{\rm true}(u)=\sum_{i=1}^{M}\ln(1+\lambda_i u)
$$
is adopted as the branch capacity coordinate, that the admissible set is defined by
$$
g_{\rm true}(u)\le\ln d_0,
$$
and that the unconstrained minimizer $u_{\mathrm{unc}}$ of the strictly convex rate objective lies strictly above the cap,
$$
u_{\mathrm{unc}}>d_0^{1/M}-1.
$$ At the PCE-Attractor, where $M=24$ and $\lambda_i=1$, the constrained minimizer is unique and satisfies
$$
u^*=d_0^{1/M}-1=8^{1/24}-1=2^{1/8}-1\approx0.0905077.
$$

*Proof.* The flat QFI spectrum of Theorem Z.5 gives
$$
g_{\rm true}(u)=M\ln(1+u).
$$
This function is continuous and strictly increasing for $u\ge0$, so the assumed inequality defines the interval
$$
0\le u\le d_0^{1/M}-1.
$$
The rate objective is strictly convex by Lemma W.1. Because its unconstrained minimizer lies above the right endpoint by hypothesis, its constrained minimizer is the right endpoint. Hence
$$
M\ln(1+u^*)=\ln d_0.
$$
Exponentiation gives $1+u^*=d_0^{1/M}$. Substitution of $d_0=8$ and $M=24$ yields
$$
u^*=8^{1/24}-1=2^{1/8}-1.
$$
The Hilbert-space Holevo bound motivates, but does not prove, the surrogate inequality assumed in this branch. ∎

**Remark Z.7c (Reset-Support-Cap Branch Boundary).** Proposition E.2a bounds the registered support of a completed reset cycle by $\ln d_0-\ln2$; it does not identify that support with the LAN/QFI capacity coordinate used in Theorem Z.7. Replacing $\ln d_0$ by $\ln d_0-\ln2$ therefore requires an additional whole-retained-output bridge and a cap-saturation certificate. On that separate branch, $d_0=8$ and $M=24$ would give
$$
u_{\mathrm{reset}}
=
\exp\!\left(\frac{\ln8-\ln2}{24}\right)-1
=
2^{1/12}-1
=
0.059463094359295\ldots.
$$
Substitution at this coordinate gives
$$
\frac{4\pi}{u_{\mathrm{reset}}}
\left(
1-\frac{u_{\mathrm{reset}}}{4\sqrt3}
+\frac{u_{\mathrm{reset}}^2}{96\sqrt3}
\operatorname{sinc}(u_{\mathrm{reset}})
\right)
=
209.521278549360\ldots
$$
for the additive core expression and
$$
\frac{4\pi}{u_{\mathrm{reset}}}
\left(1-\frac{u_{\mathrm{reset}}}{4\sqrt3}\right)
\left(
1+\frac{u_{\mathrm{reset}}^2}{96\sqrt3}
\operatorname{sinc}(u_{\mathrm{reset}})
\right)
=
209.521240001841\ldots
$$
for the multiplicative diagnostic. These are counterfactual branch values, not conclusions of Theorem Z.7.

**Corollary Z.8.2a (Capacity Shadow Price of the Bare Electromagnetic Coupling).** On the cap-active, flat-QFI branch of Theorem Z.7, the bare rate coordinate $u^*$ and the capacity shadow price are determined by the same constrained PCE problem but are distinct quantities. With
$$
\mathcal C_{\mathrm{cap}}(u)
:=
g_{\rm true}(u)-\ln d_0
=
M\ln(1+u)-\ln d_0
\le 0
$$
and
$$
\phi(u)=A_{\mathrm{PCE}}u^2-\Gamma_0 M\ln(1+u),
$$
the active boundary coordinate is
$$
u^*=d_0^{1/M}-1,
$$
while the KKT multiplier of the active capacity constraint is
$$
\zeta_{\mathrm{cap}}
=
-\frac{\phi'(u^*)}{g_{\rm true}'(u^*)}
=
\Gamma_0
-
\frac{2A_{\mathrm{PCE}}u^*(1+u^*)}{M}.
$$
On the cap-active branch $\zeta_{\mathrm{cap}}>0$. The physical Thomson-limit coupling is the normalized rate-coordinate image
$$
\alpha_{\mathrm{em}}(\mu^*)
=
\frac{u^*}{4\pi\kappa_{\mathrm{eff}}},
$$
with $\kappa_{\mathrm{eff}}$ fixed by the Ward and interface-normalization chain of Sections Z.17–Z.26.

*Proof.* Theorem Z.7 establishes that the constrained minimum is on the boundary $\mathcal C_{\mathrm{cap}}(u^*)=0$, hence
$$
M\ln(1+u^*)=\ln d_0
$$
and therefore
$$
u^*=d_0^{1/M}-1.
$$
The one-dimensional KKT stationarity equation for the Lagrangian
$$
\phi(u)+\zeta_{\mathrm{cap}}\bigl(g_{\rm true}(u)-\ln d_0\bigr)
$$
is
$$
\phi'(u^*)+\zeta_{\mathrm{cap}}g_{\rm true}'(u^*)=0.
$$
Since the flat spectrum gives
$$
g_{\rm true}'(u)=\frac{M}{1+u},
$$
and
$$
\phi'(u)=2A_{\mathrm{PCE}}u-\frac{\Gamma_0M}{1+u},
$$
substitution yields
$$
\zeta_{\mathrm{cap}}
=
-\frac{2A_{\mathrm{PCE}}u^*-\Gamma_0M/(1+u^*)}{M/(1+u^*)}
=
\Gamma_0-\frac{2A_{\mathrm{PCE}}u^*(1+u^*)}{M}.
$$
On the cap-active branch the unconstrained minimizer lies beyond the admissible capacity boundary. Since $\phi$ is strictly convex by Lemma W.1, its derivative at the lower boundary point $u^*$ is negative, hence $\zeta_{\mathrm{cap}}>0$. The final electromagnetic coupling relation is Equation (X.6) with the Appendix Z effective normalization $\kappa_{\mathrm{eff}}$. ∎

### Z.8.3 Algebraic Mass Normalization and Conditional Physical Scale

**Definition Z.8f (Algebraic Mass Normalization and Conditional Leech Mass Scale).** Define $\mu_0^{alg}:=m_P/(2\sqrt{8\varepsilon_0})$. It becomes the physical mass per unit relational information only on $\mathfrak B_{mass}$; canonical Leech calibration then gives $m(v)=\mu_0^{alg}|v|$:



$$\mu_0^{alg}:=\frac{m_P}{2\sqrt{8\varepsilon_0}}=\frac{m_P}{2\sqrt{8\ln2}}\approx0.212m_P,$$
with $\varepsilon_0=\ln2$ the structural reference.



*Conditional derivation.* On $\mathfrak B_{mass}$, Theorem N.5 assigns a system with relational information content $\mathcal I_{rel}$ the inertial mass


$$\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{rel}}(v) = |v| \quad \text{in Planck units (canonical calibration branch)}.$$

More generally, on saturated activity with boundary utilization $q$, a universal calibration $\mathcal I_{rel}(v)=\gamma|v|$ gives $m(v)=(\gamma/q)\mu_0^{alg}|v|$. Dimensionless ratios and norm ordering are independent of a common $\gamma/q$. The canonical absolute coefficient requires $\mathfrak B_{mass}$, $q=1$, and $\gamma=1$; no absolute coefficient is fixed on unsaturated activity.



**Theorem Z.8g (Conditional Energy--Norm Relation on $\mathfrak B_{mass}$).** On $\mathfrak B_{mass}$ with saturated activity of utilization $q\in(0,1]$ and a universal Leech calibration $\mathcal I_{rel}(v)=\gamma|v|$, a retained excitation labeled by $v\in\Lambda_{24}$ satisfies
$$
m(v)=\frac{\gamma}{q}\mu_0^{alg}|v|.
$$
On the canonical calibration and full-utilization branch $\gamma=q=1$,
$$
m^2(v)=(\mu_0^{alg})^2|v|^2.
$$

*Proof.* The mass bridge of Theorem N.5 gives the information-to-mass coefficient $\mu_0^{alg}/q$ on saturated activity. Substitution of $\mathcal I_{rel}(v)=\gamma|v|$ yields
$$
m(v)=\frac{\mu_0^{alg}}{q}\mathcal I_{rel}(v)
=\frac{\gamma}{q}\mu_0^{alg}|v|.
$$
Setting $\gamma=q=1$ and squaring proves the canonical formula. Unsaturated activity supplies no absolute coefficient. ∎





**Remark Z.8g.3 (Complete Branch Dependence of the Absolute Mass Map).** Leech rootlessness fixes the norm gap and $\gamma$ fixes norm-to-information calibration, while $\mathfrak B_{mass}$ fixes the information-to-mass coefficient. On saturated activity with utilization $q$, absolute masses and gaps scale as $\gamma/q$; on unsaturated activity no absolute coefficient is asserted. Dimensionless shell ratios remain independent of a universal common factor.



**Corollary Z.8g.1 (Conditional Physical Mass Gap).** On $\mathfrak B_{mass}$ with canonical $q=\gamma=1$, the Leech norm gap gives the physical mass gap:

$$\Delta_{gap}=\mu_0^{alg}\sqrt{|v|_{min}^2}=2\mu_0^{alg}.$$

The factor of 2 arises from the Leech lattice's minimum squared norm $|v|^2_{\min} = 4$ (Theorem Z.8c).

**Remark Z.8g.2: Scope of the Gap.** Leech geometry alone fixes the dimensionless norm gap $|v|_{min}=2$. On $\mathfrak B_{mass}$ it becomes $2\mu_0^{alg}c^2$ whenever $\gamma/q=1$; the canonical normalization used in Corollary Z.8g.1 is $q=\gamma=1$. Landauer and rootlessness alone do not fix that absolute energy.




### Z.8.4 Shell-$J^{PC}$ Correspondence

**Theorem Z.8h (Shell-$J^{PC}$ and Mass Readout on the Canonical Shell-Assignment Branch).** On the canonical shell-assignment branch, prescribe
$$
\Delta_J(0)=0,
\qquad
\Delta_J(J)=J(J-1)\quad(J\ge1),
$$
$$
\Delta_P(+)=\Delta_C(+)=0,
\qquad
\Delta_P(-)=4,
\qquad
\Delta_C(-)=8.
$$
The branch assigns
$$
|v|^2(J^{PC})
=
a^2+\Delta_J(J)+\Delta_P(P)+\Delta_C(C)
$$
and uses the dimensionless mass readout
$$
\frac{m(J^{PC})}{m(0^{++})}
=
\sqrt{\frac{|v|^2(J^{PC})}{4}}
\sqrt{1+\frac{J(J+1)}{a(2b-a)}}.
$$
On the minimal branch,
$$
a=2,
\qquad
b=6,
\qquad
M=2ab=24,
$$
so
$$
a^2=4=|v|_{\min}^2,
\qquad
a(2b-a)=M-a^2=20,
\qquad
d_{\mathrm{Golay}}=8.
$$
The equalities $a^2=|v|_{\min}^2=4$, $M=24$, and $d_{\mathrm{Golay}}=8$ follow from Theorems Z.1, Z.5, Z.8c, and Z.13b. Assigning $4$ to parity, $8$ to charge conjugation, $J(J-1)$ to spin, and $M-a^2=20$ as the denominator of the spin readout are prescribed branch rules; they are not consequences of the Leech or Golay theorems.

| Entry | Value | Status |
|:---|:---|:---|
| Base numerical scale | $a^2=4=|v|_{\min}^2$ | Derived numerical equality; physical $0^{++}$ identification is a branch assignment |
| Spin increment | $\Delta_J(J)=J(J-1)$ | Branch rule |
| Parity increment | $\Delta_P(-)=4$ | Branch assignment of the derived scale $4$ |
| C-parity increment | $\Delta_C(-)=8$ | Branch assignment of the derived scale $8$ |
| Spin denominator | $a(2b-a)=20$ | Derived arithmetic value; its use in the mass readout is a branch rule |

*Proof.* Theorems Z.1 and Z.5 give $(a,b,M)=(2,6,24)$, Theorem Z.8c gives $|v|_{\min}^2=4$, and Theorem Z.13b gives $d_{\mathrm{Golay}}=8$. Hence $a^2=4$ and $a(2b-a)=M-a^2=20$. Substitution of the prescribed increments gives the displayed shell assignment, and substitution of that shell into the prescribed readout gives the displayed mass ratio. No representation-theoretic map from $J^{PC}$ to $\operatorname{Aut}(\Lambda_{24})$ is used or proved. ∎

**Corollary Z.8h.1 (Conditional Ordering of the Prescribed Costs).** Within the canonical shell-assignment branch,
$$
\Delta_C(-)>\Delta_P(-)>\Delta_J(J)
\qquad(J=0,1,2),
$$
because $8>4>2\ge0$. This is an arithmetic ordering of the prescribed increments. It does not establish a branch-independent physical ordering of glueball masses.

*Proof.* Direct substitution gives $\Delta_C(-)=8$, $\Delta_P(-)=4$, $\Delta_J(2)=2$, and $\Delta_J(1)=\Delta_J(0)=0$. ∎

**Arithmetic check.**

| State | $J$ | $P$ | $C$ | $\Delta_J$ | $\Delta_P$ | $\Delta_C$ | Assigned shell |
|:---|---:|:---:|:---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| $0^{++}$ | 0 | + | + | 0 | 0 | 0 | 4 |
| $2^{++}$ | 2 | + | + | 2 | 0 | 0 | 6 |
| $0^{-+}$ | 0 | - | + | 0 | 4 | 0 | 8 |
| $3^{++}$ | 3 | + | + | 6 | 0 | 0 | 10 |

The table verifies substitution into the branch rule; it is not an empirical determination of Leech shell labels.

**Remark Z.8h.2 (Derived Inventory and Assignment Boundary).** The derived numerical inventory is
$$
(a,b,M,|v|_{\min}^2,d_{\mathrm{Golay}})
=
(2,6,24,4,8).
$$
The physical maps
$$
J\mapsto\Delta_J(J),
\qquad
P\mapsto\Delta_P(P),
\qquad
C\mapsto\Delta_C(C),
$$
and the spin-corrected mass readout are additional branch data. The Landauer, Leech, and Golay results supply the numerical scales but do not assign those scales to glueball quantum numbers.

**Theorem Z.8h.3 (Reassignment No-Go for the Pinned Discrete Scales).** Let $\mathcal A$ be the class of additive shell rules
$$
|v|^2(J^{PC})
=
4+\Delta_J(J)+\Delta_P(P)+\Delta_C(C)
$$
with
$$
\Delta_P(+)=\Delta_C(+)=0,
\qquad
\{\Delta_P(-),\Delta_C(-)\}=\{4,8\},
$$
and $\Delta_J(J)\in2\mathbb Z_{\ge0}$ with $\Delta_J(0)=0$. Retain the branch mass readout
$$
R_J(s)
=
\sqrt{\frac{s}{4}}
\sqrt{1+\frac{J(J+1)}{20}}.
$$
For the Athenodorou–Teper [2020, Table 17] marginal statistical data
$$
m_0=3.405\pm0.021,
\qquad
m_{0^{-+}}=5.276\pm0.045,
\qquad
m_{2^{-+}}=6.32\pm0.09,
$$
define the diagonal ratio error by
$$
\sigma_{\mathrm{diag}}\left(\frac{m}{m_0}\right)
=
\frac{m}{m_0}
\sqrt{
\left(\frac{\sigma_m}{m}\right)^2
+
\left(\frac{\sigma_{m_0}}{m_0}\right)^2
}.
$$
This gives
$$
r(0^{-+})=1.549486\pm0.016309,
\qquad
r(2^{-+})=1.856094\pm0.028804.
$$
Every rule in $\mathcal A$ has at least one of these states with absolute diagonal statistical pull greater than $4$.

*Proof.* At fixed $J$, $R_J(s)$ is strictly increasing in $s$. For $2^{-+}$ the admissible shell is even and at least $8$. The first three values are
$$
R_2(8)=1.612452,
\qquad
R_2(10)=1.802776,
\qquad
R_2(12)=1.974842,
$$
with diagonal pulls
$$
-8.46,
\qquad
-1.85,
\qquad
+4.12.
$$
All shells $s\ge12$ have pull at least $+4.12$ by monotonicity. Thus the only shell with absolute pull at most $4$ is $s=10$, which forces
$$
\Delta_P(-)=4,
\qquad
\Delta_J(2)=2,
\qquad
\Delta_C(-)=8.
$$
On that branch $0^{-+}$ has shell $8$ and prediction $\sqrt2=1.414214$, giving pull $-8.29$. If $\Delta_P(-)=8$, then $2^{-+}$ has shell at least $12$ and pull at least $+4.12$. ∎

**Remark Z.8h.3a (Covariance-Robust Statistical Bound).** The preceding statement uses diagonal propagation because the source does not publish covariance with the common $0^{++}$ denominator. Under first-order propagation with any correlation coefficient $\rho\in[-1,1]$, the ratio uncertainty is at most
$$
\sigma_{\max}(m/m_0)
=
\frac{m}{m_0}
\left(
\frac{\sigma_m}{m}
+
\frac{\sigma_{m_0}}{m_0}
\right).
$$
This gives
$$
\sigma_{\max}(0^{-+})=0.02277,
\qquad
\sigma_{\max}(2^{-+})=0.03788.
$$
If $\Delta_P(-)=4$ and $\Delta_J(2)=2$, the $0^{-+}$ deficit remains $5.9\sigma_{\max}$. For every other allowed spin increment, the $2^{-+}$ discrepancy is at least $3.1\sigma_{\max}$. If $\Delta_P(-)=8$, the same $2^{-+}$ bound applies. This is a conservative first-order bound and does not incorporate unreported systematic uncertainty.

**Remark Z.8h.4 (Retirement and Reinstatement Conditions for the Shell-Assignment Branch).** Against the declared continuum benchmark, the canonical shell-assignment and mass-readout branch is rejected and retired from the falsifiable-prediction registry. The independent structural equalities
$$
a=2,
\qquad
b=6,
\qquad
M=24,
\qquad
a^2=|v|_{\min}^2=4,
\qquad
d_{\mathrm{Golay}}=8
$$
remain unchanged. The physical identification of $0^{++}$ with the minimum shell, the assignments $\Delta_P(-)=4$ and $\Delta_C(-)=8$, the rule $\Delta_J(J)=J(J-1)$, the spin denominator in the mass readout, and the physical interpretation of Corollary Z.8h.1 belong to the retired branch.

Since $\operatorname{Aut}(\Lambda_{24})$ is finite, a nontrivial continuous embedding $SO(3)\hookrightarrow\operatorname{Aut}(\Lambda_{24})$ is impossible. Reinstatement requires a finite subgroup $G_{\mathrm{rot}}\le SO(3)$, an injective homomorphism
$$
G_{\mathrm{rot}}\hookrightarrow\operatorname{Aut}(\Lambda_{24}),
$$
a declared continuum-spin reconstruction or subduction rule, and an orbit-to-shell mass readout fixed before comparison with a new held-out continuum dataset. The existing ratio $m(0^{-+})/m(0^{++})=1.549(16)$ is a benchmark, not a preregistered prediction. Theorem Z.8k does not depend on the retired shell assignment or mass readout.
